- [ ] 2026-08-14 · C · 🧮 **THE ADP WE READ AS "THE MARKET TODAY" IS A CUMULATIVE MEAN OVER A MONTH OF DRAFTS, AND ITS STABILITY IS ARITHMETIC RATHER THAN EVIDENCE.** Measured on my own archive, and it changes how `adp` should be read by anything that treats it as a current opinion.
  **WHAT I MEASURED FIRST, and nearly reported as good news:** inside the top 150, MFL's published ADP barely moves day to day — median |change| **0.21 picks** (08-11→12) and **0.17** (08-12→13), largest single move 3.3. Read naively that says the market has settled and the choice of snapshot hardly matters.
  **THEN I CHECKED WHY.** `total_drafts` goes **115 → 119 → 125**: each day adds **4 and then 6** drafts, i.e. **3.4% and 4.8%** of the running total. **The published figure is a mean over an ever-growing sample, so one day's new drafts carry about 4% weight by construction.** A near-zero daily change is what that arithmetic produces whatever the drafters do.
  **THE INFERENCE, LABELLED AS ONE:** if the published mean moves ~0.2 picks at ~4% weight, the movement implied *in the new drafts* is of order **5 picks** — roughly twenty-five times what the headline suggests. That is an order-of-magnitude derivation, not a measurement, because MFL publishes only the cumulative average and not the marginal day.
  **TWO CONSEQUENCES, and the second is the one I would act on.** (1) **F5's "latest snapshot strictly before the draft" matters MORE than the raw numbers imply**, not less — the published figure hides movement rather than showing there is none. (2) **Anything reading `adp` as "where the market thinks he goes now" is reading an average that includes drafts from July.** For a draft on 22 August that is systematically stale in a way no staleness check can catch, because the number updates every day while its content is mostly old — a measurement about the present computed from an accumulation of the past.
  **NOT A CHANGE REQUEST AND NOT A RECOMMENDATION TO STOP USING IT.** `survival` needs a central estimate and this is the best one available; the point is that its DAY-TO-DAY STABILITY must not be read as market consensus, and `total_drafts` — which the per-source archive now records — is what makes the damping visible. No fix is possible on this provider: a rolling-window source would be a different capture, and that is a September question with the data in hand rather than a pre-draft one.

Failed and empty sources are now dropped and NAMED before they reach the intersection.   **REHEARSED END TO END WITHOUT EGRESS against the real pick record**: all three seasons grade cleanly, keepers out at 30/23/20, and a deliberately tight synthetic source returns **rho +0.98** against a noisy one at **+0.65** — so the sign is the right way up, which is the thing that caught me the first time (I copied `-adp` from `exp_source_grade`, where the outcome is realized POINTS, and got **−0.81 for a source that predicts almost perfectly**).
  **WHAT YOU WILL GET IS SOFTER THAN A COEFFICIENT LOOKS, and both reasons are printed WITH the table rather than filed beside it, because both push every rho the same way — up.** (1) A year-scoped ADP is the ACCUMULATED season and contains drafts later than ours, unequally per source. (2) **2024 and 2025 are pooled**, so a team that changed coordinator between them is averaged across two regimes — splitting by season is one argument away if you want it. (3) `NEUTRAL_MARGIN = 14` was declared before the data was seen and is written in the module rather than fitted; I have not tuned it and would not without a pre-registration.
  **⚠ AND ONE THING WORTH KNOWING SEPARATELY: THE pbp RELEASE HAS 2025.** Your durability work and mine both hit `import_weekly_data` 404ing for 2025 — the season closest to the board. Median per-player ratio 2.21.** `draft/backtest/projection_spread_vs_realized.json`. Measurement only; `projections.py` is yours and I have not touched it.
  | pos | n | board `weekly_sd` | measured (missed weeks = 0) | ratio |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | QB | 20 | 19.49 | 10.83 | **1.80x** |
  | RB | 42 | 17.96 | 7.58 | **2.37x** |
  | TE | 17 | 14.97 | 6.26 | **2.39x** |
  | WR | 51 | 14.89 | 7.09 | **2.10x** |
  **THE MECHANISM, TO THE LINE, AND IT IS CRITERION 1 RATHER THAN AN ARITHMETIC SLIP.** `projections.py:244` sets `weekly_sd = season_sd / sqrt(games)` where `season_sd = mean_proj * var`, and `var` comes from `player_variance` — whose reasons are **season-level role risk**: *"second year"*, *"behind on the depth chart"*, *"carrying Questionable"* (38 of the 146 draftable carry no reason at all, 10 each carry the first two). **Dividing a season-total uncertainty by sqrt(games) turns it into a per-week number as if every bit of season uncertainty were independent weekly noise.** It is not — role risk resolves once, in September, not afresh every Sunday. The two ends are not denominated in the same thing, and the ratio is the size of the gap.
  **WHY IT MATTERS RATHER THAN BEING A LABELLING QUIBBLE: CONSUMERS USE IT AS WEEKLY VOLATILITY.** `cory_conditional.py:361` is `var = sum(p["weekly_sd"] ** 2 for p in starters)` — summing weekly variances across a starting lineup. At 2.2x on the sd that is **~4.9x on the variance**, so a simulated week's spread comes out about **2.2x too wide**. Everything downstream that rewards upside — the money proxy, participation, ceiling terms — is running on roughly double the true week-to-week dispersion, which systematically **flatters high-variance strategies**: the wider the simulated distribution, the better "chase the ceiling" looks against "take the steady floor".
  **I MEASURED IT THE WAY THAT FAVOURS THE BOARD.** A missed week scores zero in a real lineup, so the comparison adds a 0.0 for every week the availability record says the player was out (byes excluded — a bye is not a scoring opportunity). **107 of the 130 missed at least one week**, and including those zeros raises the measured figure at every position (RB 7.07 → 7.58, QB 9.65 → 10.83). The ratios above are the post-correction, board-favouring ones.
  **⚠ AND I NEARLY REPORTED THE ZERO-INCLUSIVE FIGURES AS IDENTICAL TO THE PLAYED-ONLY ONES.** My first pass compared MEDIANS — and the median player misses nothing, so the median cannot move however many zeros the tail carries. It reported "0 of 130 missed a week" beside a McCaffrey who missed twelve. The lookup was also wrong underneath: `missed` is keyed by **int**, and I had read `json.dumps`'s quoted rendering as evidence of string keys. Both were caught by a number that could not be true, which is the only reason they were caught at all.
  **THREE LIMITS.** (1) **K and DEF are EXCLUDED, not measured**: `nflverse_weekly_to_scoring` scores every kicker week at 0.0 through this path, so kicker volatility is unmeasured and the earlier "K sd 0.00" I might have quoted means nothing. (2) **Survivorship** — the cut conditions on being priced inside pick 150 in 2026, so vanished careers are absent. (3) Realized past volatility is not expected 2026 volatility; it is the best estimate available and the gap is far larger than estimation error.
  **NOT A CHANGE REQUEST, AND SPECIFICALLY NOT "MULTIPLY BY 0.45".** The artifact carries per-player `measured_weekly_sd_with_missed_as_zero` so a per-player prior is available if you want one, but the honest reading is that `weekly_sd` and `season_sd` are answering two different questions and a scalar cannot reconcile them — the season figure may well be right FOR THE SEASON while the weekly one is wrong for the week. **Six days from the draft I would not re-fit anything; I would want to know whether any pre-draft decision actually reads `weekly_sd`, and that is your call to make.**

  **DURATION IS FLAT AND THE SAMPLE IS LARGE.** Mean COMPLETED absence, league-wide: **QB 3.67 · RB 3.35 · TE 3.24 · WR 3.12 weeks** — a 0.55-week spread across four positions on 320 to 1,111 spells each.   | RB | 104 | 0.663 | 1.68 | 51.0% |
  | QB | 54 | 0.519 | 1.56 | 66.7% |
  **A draftable WR gets through a season unscathed 41.7% of the time; a draftable RB 51.0%.** That is the whole of the WR-below-RB availability result, and it is a frequency effect end to end.
  **WHAT IT MEANS FOR A MODEL, and it is not "lower the WR constant".** A single `games_expected` per position cannot express *"misses often, briefly"* versus *"rarely, but when he does it ends the season"* — and those are different draft-day risks even at identical expected games. **The right shape is a per-position hazard (how likely is a spell) crossed with a near-common duration (~3.2 weeks, plus a ~22% chance it never ends).** The duration half being flat is what makes that tractable: one duration distribution, four rates.
  **AND IT QUANTIFIES THE SURVIVORSHIP I FLAGGED.** League-wide, WRs have clean seasons **24.7%** of the time; inside pick 150 it is **41.7%**. The draftable cut is conditioned on having survived, so every availability figure I have sent you — mine included — is optimistic by roughly that much. That is an argument for reading the ORDERING rather than the levels, which is where I have been pointing throughout.
  **⚠ THE DRAFTABLE SPELL COUNTS ARE SMALL — QB 28 spells, TE 31.** The duration numbers I would trust are the league-wide ones; the frequency table above rests on 42 to 139 player-seasons per position, which is enough for the ordering and not enough for a third decimal place. **Nothing wired, `projections.py` is yours.**

- [x] 2026-08-14 · C · ✅ **CAVEAT 1 ON THE DURABILITY CARD IS NOW MOSTLY LIFTED, AND CAVEAT 3 IS GONE ENTIRELY.** I wrote both of them; this is me retiring them with measurements rather than leaving them to be worked around. They are the two that told you to distrust the numbers.
  **CAVEAT 3 — *"2025 IS ABSENT AND IT IS THE SEASON CLOSEST TO THE BOARD"* — WAS WRONG.** 2025 was published the whole time. `import_weekly_data` 404s because it asks for a release named `player_stats`; nflverse calls it **`stats_player`**. All three seasons are now in the artifact. The release names are pinned in `nflverse_release.py` with a test on the exact string and a `describe_failure` that reports the asset WE asked for, so a 404 can never again be read as a season that does not exist.
  **CAVEAT 1 — *"`missed` is WEEKS WITH NO PRODUCTION, not WEEKS INJURED; a healthy backup and an inactive one are indistinguishable"* — was true of the SOURCE, not of the question.** The weekly **roster** release carries a per-week `status`: `ACT` 82,106 · `DEV` 26,108 (practice squad) · `RES` 16,142 (IR) · `INA` 10,773 (gameday scratch). **A healthy backup who dresses is `ACT`, and the old method counted him ABSENT because he recorded nothing.** Today's numbers are built on `status == ACT`.
  **HOW BIG THAT MIS-COUNT WAS, AND IT IS UNEVEN BY POSITION — which is what makes it dangerous rather than merely imprecise.** Share of ACTIVE player-weeks with no stat row, league-wide: **QB 39.4% · TE 27.4% · RB 13.8% · WR 12.9%.** Those are exactly the weeks the production-based method scored as absences. A positional comparison built on it inherits a bias three times larger at quarterback than at receiver — **it would manufacture a positional ordering out of usage patterns.**
  **WHAT IS *NOT* LIFTED, stated precisely rather than rounded up to "fixed":** a player **scratched** for coaching reasons is `INA`, so he still counts as absent. The conflation is reduced to healthy-scratches only, not eliminated. **A benched player who dresses is now correctly present; a benched player who does not dress is still counted as missing.**
  **AND IT BARELY MOVED THE HEADLINE, which is the reassuring part.** Inside pick 150 the source change is worth **≤ 0.27 games** at every position (largest at QB and TE, exactly where the no-stat rate is highest) — because these are starters, not backups. So the earlier answer was not badly wrong; it was resting on a foundation that could not be defended, and now it is. **Your four verdicts that turned on 2.1 vs 2.7 weeks can be re-read against `absence_shape` in the artifact, where duration and frequency are separated.**

- [ ] 2026-08-14 · C · 🧩 **THE TWO SPREAD FINDINGS ARE ONE MECHANISM, AND THE ARITHMETIC CLOSES. Week-to-week noise is only ~11% of season projection variance; `weekly_sd = season_sd / sqrt(games)` assumes 100%.** This supersedes my "weekly_sd is 2.2x too wide" note by explaining it — and it also says the SEASON figure is wrong in the OPPOSITE direction.
  **THE MEASUREMENT.** Weekly noise as a share of season projection variance: **QB 8.5% · RB 8.5% · WR 12.2% · TE 12.6%** (median 10.9% over 129 draftable players). The other ~89% is season-level — role, injury, team, usage — and none of it scales as sqrt(games).
  **TWO ERRORS COMPOSE, IN OPPOSITE DIRECTIONS:**
  | pos | board `var` | measured | season too NARROW by | weekly share | **predicted** weekly overstatement | **observed** |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | QB | 0.220 | 0.432 | 0.51x | 8.5% | **1.74x** | 1.89x |
  | RB | 0.388 | 0.615 | 0.63x | 8.5% | **2.17x** | 2.41x |
  | TE | 0.382 | 0.469 | 0.81x | 12.6% | **2.29x** | 2.31x |
  | WR | 0.333 | 0.412 | 0.81x | 12.2% | **2.31x** | 2.16x |
  **THE PREDICTION MATCHES THE OBSERVATION AT ALL FOUR POSITIONS WITHIN 8%, and that is what makes it one account rather than two coincidences.** The board is **too CONFIDENT about the season** (its `var` is half to four-fifths of measured projection error) and **too UNCERTAIN about the week** (~2.2x the realized volatility), and both fall out of the same line: `projections.py:244` divides a season-total uncertainty by sqrt(games) as though every bit of it were independent weekly noise, when a tenth of it is.
  **⚠ SO NEITHER IS A SCALING ERROR AND NO CONSTANT FIXES IT.** Multiplying `weekly_sd` by 0.45 would leave a season figure that is still too narrow; widening `var` would make the weekly figure worse. The season and weekly quantities need to be derived independently, because measurement says they are ~89% independent.
  **✅ AND THE REPLACEMENT FOR THE SEASON HALF IS ALREADY IN THE REPO, WRITTEN AND UNWIRED.** `projection_error.proj_sd_for` applies a **measured** per-(position, rank-band) `sd_ratio` from `projection_error_calibration.json` — **1,304 graded players, 20 measured cells** — and **nothing calls it.** That is my lane's rule-14 gap and it is the thing that would move this. It is not a drop-in: **8 of the 146 draftable sit on bands it calls `unmeasurable`, and it returns None there by design rather than a fallback constant** (its own docstring: *"a fallback is exactly how 0.25 * proj_mean reached the board"*). **What to do for those 8 is a decision, and it is yours.**
  **⚠ I ALSO NEARLY ROUTED THIS AS A DEFECT IN `proj_sd_for` ITSELF.** Reading the calibration with `json.load` returns string keys `"QB|17-32"` while `_cell` looks up the tuple `("QB", "17-32")`, so every player came back `unmeasurable` — 145 of 146, which is what made me look. **The module is right: `projection_error.load()` splits the keys back, and I had bypassed it.** Reading what actually calls it is the only reason that is a note here rather than a false alarm in your inbox.
  **⚠ AND THE CALIBRATION UNDERSTATES ITSELF.** Its own header declares it optimistic — players with no weekly rows at all are excluded, so every band is fitted on players who reached the field. **Measured season spread is if anything wider, which makes the board's too-narrow season figure worse, not better.**

- [ ] 2026-08-14 · C · 🏈 **nflverse ADDED `fumbles_lost_total` AND THE THREE SPLIT COLUMNS NO LONGER CARRY EVERYTHING — `grade._FUM_LOST_COLS` misses 14% of fumbles lost.** Found by cross-checking the weekly points store against a fresh read of the same seasons, which is the only reason it surfaced at all. **Not urgent, nothing shipped is wrong, and I have not touched `grade.py`** — it carries no `# TERRITORY:` header and it is grading infrastructure, so it is yours to place.
  **THE DEFECT, TO THE ROW.** `grade.py:46` is `_FUM_LOST_COLS = ("rushing_fumbles_lost", "receiving_fumbles_lost", "sack_fumbles_lost")`. A real 2025 week-1 row (sleeper `11306`, gsis `00-0038496`) reads **`rushing_fumbles_lost: 0 · receiving_fumbles_lost: 0 · sack_fumbles_lost: 0 · fumbles_lost_total: 1`.** The mapper returns `fum_lost: 0` and the player scores 0.0 where he should score −2.0.
  **IT IS SYSTEMATIC AND STABLE, NOT A ONE-OFF ROW:** fumbles lost invisible to the split read are **2023: 40 of 304 (13%) · 2024: 39 of 271 (14%) · 2025: 36 of 249 (14%)** — **115 of 824 across three seasons.** About one fumble in seven, every year. The likely cause is fumbles on plays that are none of rush/reception/sack (returns, laterals), which the total column now catches and the splits never did.
  **✅ NOTHING ON DISK IS WRONG, AND THAT IS THE FIRST THING TO SAY.** I cross-checked **5,010 player-weeks** of `nflverse_weekly_points_2025.json` against a fresh read: **97.2% agree exactly** (median absolute difference 0.0000), and every one of the 141 disagreements has the STORE right and the fresh read wrong — 118 of them exactly **−2.00**. The store was built when the split columns still carried these, or through the pbp recovery path that emits our keys directly. **This is schema drift that bites on RE-READ, not a historical error, so no grade already computed is affected.**
  **✅ AND IT DOES NOT MOVE THE SPREAD FINDING I SENT YOU AN HOUR AGO.** I re-ran the whole weekly-volatility measurement with `fum_lost = max(splits, fumbles_lost_total)`: **QB 10.83 → 10.84, RB 7.43 → 7.43, TE 6.26 → 6.26, WR 7.01 → 7.01.** At most 0.01. The 2.2x over-statement stands unchanged; I checked before leaving it standing rather than after.
  **THE FIX IS ONE LINE AND I HAVE DELIBERATELY NOT WRITTEN IT** — `fum_lost = max(sum of splits, fumbles_lost_total)` rather than either alone, because a plain switch to the total would double-count on the older frames where the splits ARE populated and `add()` accumulates. **Both vocabularies have to keep working: the mapper's own docstring is about exactly that, and it is the reason this needs your judgement rather than my patch.**

- [ ] 2026-08-14 · C · 🛑 **DO NOT WIRE PACE AS A DRAFT-DAY TIEBREAK — I measured whether it predicts anything and it does not. This walks back the actionability of my OWN pace routing from two hours ago.** I gave you a measured artifact and said the consumers were yours; before you spend time on them, here is the evidence that they should not exist.
  **THE LAGGED TEST, which is the shape of the actual draft-day question** — prior-season usage and prior-season team pace predicting the FOLLOWING season's points, 588 player-season pairs:
  | pos | n | prior share alone | share x neutral pace | delta | **pace ALONE** |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | RB | 167 | +0.686 | +0.686 | **−0.0001** | **+0.057** |
  | WR | 244 | +0.650 | +0.651 | **+0.0011** | **+0.071** |
  | TE | 106 | +0.620 | +0.628 | **+0.0077** | **+0.056** |
  **Pace alone predicts next-season points at rho 0.06.** As a multiplier on usage share it moves the correlation by a thousandth. At n=106 a delta of 0.008 is indistinguishable from noise.
  **AND THE REASON IS CLEAN: TEAM PACE BARELY PERSISTS.** Year-over-year across all 32 teams, neutral pace **2023→2024 rho +0.194 · 2024→2025 rho +0.024** (raw is worse: +0.165 and **−0.065**). **A team's pace this year says almost nothing about its pace next year**, so a draft-day tiebreak on last season's pace is using a number that will not describe the team being drafted. That single fact explains the whole null.
  **⚠ THE SAME-SEASON VERSION LOOKS LIKE A STRONGER TEST AND IS A WEAKER ONE.** Contemporaneous share predicts points at rho 0.91-0.96 and pace adds ±0.002 — but same-season share is an OUTCOME, not a prior, and you do not have it on draft day. I ran it first, saw the null, and did not report it, because the honest question is the lagged one and it could in principle have answered differently.
  **✅ WHAT SURVIVES, AND IT IS NOT NOTHING.** (1) The raw-vs-neutral finding stands entirely — **rho 0.53 between the two orderings**, DAL #1 raw and #17 neutral. If pace is ever used for anything, the neutral figure is the one to use and the raw one is actively misleading. (2) **IN-SEASON use is untouched by this**: there you know the current team's current pace and no persistence is required. Nothing measured here speaks against that.

  ```

Cory read my earlier note and said "this is a problem with our model and it needs to be found and fixed" — I have found the mechanism, and I am NOT claiming the model is wrong. Here is exactly what is established and what is not.** Median rank delta, non-QB inside pick 150, positive = the market takes him LATER:
  ```
  pos   n  |  vs our ADP column (FantasyPros)  |  vs OUR MODEL (vorp)
           |       MFL            FFC          |     MFL         FFC
  WR   55  |      -1.0           -4.0          |     0.0        -4.0
  RB   45  |      +7.0           +2.0          |    -4.0       -10.0
  TE   16  |     -11.0          +21.5          |    -2.0       +34.0
  ```
  **THE RB SIGN FLIPS BETWEEN OUR TWO ORDERINGS.** Our `adp` column ranks RBs EARLIER than both markets; our `vorp` ranks them LATER than both. The markets are BETWEEN our two columns. This is not "the market disagrees with us" — **it is our board disagreeing with itself, and the engine scores on `vorp`.**
  **THE MECHANISM, READ OFF `vorp.replacement_levels` DIAGNOSTICS RATHER THAN INFERRED.** The greedy flex allocator gives each of the 10 FLEX slots to whichever eligible position has the best next-man-up RAW `proj_mean`. Result on the shipped board: **`starter_counts` RB 21, WR 29, TE 10 — nine of ten flex slots to WR, one to RB, none to TE.** So RB replacement sits at the 21st RB (189.02) and WR at the 29th WR (173.17). A shallower replacement rank means a HIGHER replacement value, and VORP is `proj − replacement`, so this depresses every RB's VORP and lifts every WR's. That is precisely the direction our `vorp` disagrees with both markets.
  ⚖️ **WHAT I CANNOT TELL YOU, AND I WILL NOT GUESS: WHICH SIDE IS RIGHT.** Two readings fit every number above equally well. (1) The flex rule under-rates RBs, and `vorp` is wrong. (2) Real drafters over-draft running backs — a famous market bias — and `vorp` is RIGHT while both markets are biased. ❗ **ADP data cannot separate these, because both hypotheses predict exactly this table.** What separates them is REALIZED POINTS by draft slot: did RBs taken at those slots actually outscore the WRs available there? That is a backtest against `nflverse` weekly points, not an ADP comparison, and it is a real piece of work rather than a check.
  🎯 **THE PART THAT IS ACTIONABLE BEFORE 08-22 REGARDLESS OF WHO IS RIGHT:** the war room surfaces both `adp` and `vorp`, and on running backs they disagree by about eleven rank slots. **Cory will read two numbers on the same card that say different things about the same player, and neither is labelled as the odd one out.** That is a surface-contract problem you and B own, and it does not wait on resolving the valuation question.
  ⚠️ **AND A CORRECTION TO MY OWN ROUTE THREE ITEMS UP.** I sent you the wopr gradient as "the most draft-actionable thing I have measured today". **It does not survive a split by position** — pooled it is -0.39 with the two markets agreeing at +0.04; within WR it is -0.04/+0.188, within RB -0.04/-0.472, within TE +0.177/-0.451, and the markets stop agreeing with each other entirely. It was Simpson's paradox: wopr separated the groups because WRs have high wopr and RBs low. **The position effect above is what was actually underneath it.** `market_agreement` now ships `by_position` and `survives_within_position` beside the pooled figure so that reading cannot be made again — including by me.

En route: endDraft now re-seeds keepers (they are placements, not picks). · 2026-08-17 · B · 🔴🔴 **FOUND DURING THE ITEM-2 REHEARSAL, AND IT IS SERIOUS: THE DRAFT-NIGHT FALLBACK CLOCK BREAKS ON YOUR VERY FIRST PICK IN MANUAL MODE.** Not inferred — reproduced step by step through the actual UI (`.wrv-take`, real clicks, real console), not a blind script loop; I checked the naive version of this test first, found it was clicking a stale reference, and rebuilt it before trusting anything.
  **THE SYMPTOM, verified live:** in manual/rehearsal mode (no Sleeper sync — the exact draft-night fallback path, per `app.js`'s own comment: *"Manual mode is the DRAFT-NIGHT FALLBACK, so this was the one path that had to work when sync failed"*), taking my first pick fires `console.error('[pick-state] [roster marked] 1 picks != [my_picks < clock] 0 made — the need model is reading MORE picks than the clock')` — and it repeats, unchanged, on every subsequent pick (verified through 3 real picks, plus 9 opponent picks marked `Gone` in between, which does not clear it either). **It is also visibly wrong on screen, not just in the console:** the WATCH strip's `Starters: N/9` correctly advances (3/9 → 4/9 → 5/9 as I took real players) while `12 picks left` on the SAME line never moves, through all three picks. Two numbers on one banner, one updating correctly and one frozen — the exact class of contradiction this whole project has been hunting for weeks.
  **THE MECHANISM, traced to source:** `currentPick()` (`app.js:1669`) has special-case pre-draft handling (`if (ps.pickEvents === 0) return mine[0]` — correctly anchors to my real first pick, e.g. 33, before anything is recorded) but the moment `pickEvents` becomes non-zero — i.e., the instant I take my FIRST pick — it falls through to `ps.currentPick`, which `pickState()` (`app.js:1511`) defines as `pickEvents + 1`. That treats the count of MY locally-recorded picks as if it were the room's absolute pick number, discarding the 32 picks that happened before my seat's turn. So after pick 1, `currentPick` becomes `1 + 1 = 2` instead of continuing from 33 — and `myPicksLeft()`/`accounting.js`'s `picksMade` (`myPicks.filter(p => p < currentPick)`) then compares my real pick numbers (33, 48, 68…) against a clock stuck near 2, so **`picksMade` reads 0 forever, no matter how many picks you actually take.** Confirmed this is manual-only: `state.sync` present uses `state.sync.currentPickNumber() - 1`, the real room position, and does not have this bug — it's specific to the unsynced fallback.
  **WHY IT MATTERS BEYOND THE ONE VISIBLE LINE:** `myPicksLeft()` feeds `DraftLegality.assess` (`app.js:7229`/`7544`), and the same `picksMade`/`picksLeft` pair feeds the `mustDraftNow`/`onesieSqueeze` endgame logic in `legality.js`. If the clock never advances past ~2, every one of those calculations is running on a permanently-wrong "how many picks do I have left" — which is exactly the number that decides when the tool tells you to stop waiting on a onesie and draft it now. **This isn't cosmetic; it can change what the tool tells you to do, specifically in the mode that exists for when the real connection fails on draft night.**
  **ASK:** this needs `app.js`, which is on hold tonight for the stack/handcuff merge — flagging now rather than waiting, given what it's the fallback FOR. **DEFAULT:** none from me; not proposing a fix since I don't know whether the intended contract is "pickEvents counts from my anchor pick" or something else — that's a design call in your file, not mine to guess at.

We had not: `own_model_v5.py`'s `pace_lam` is regression-to-the-mean on prior-season team target/rush VOLUME, inert at QB and RB and a no-op at WR (λ=1.00); `own_model_v6.py` contains the string zero times. Now measured from real nflverse play-by-play. **The shape of the finding, which is more useful than "null":** what everybody MEANS by pace — plays per game — **does not carry year to year** (+0.062 [−0.154, +0.265]); what does carry is **tempo, seconds per play** (+0.417 [+0.191, +0.602], more persistent than the week-1 implied team total at +0.302); tempo **is** orthogonal to implied total (−0.140 [−0.358, +0.077]), so the premise held; and it **still** does not pay because `tempo(Y−1) → plays(Y)` cannot be resolved from zero (−0.181 [−0.368, +0.014]) — measured without reading one fantasy point. Graded anyway on `own_v6`'s own cells: bar not cleared, and the **negative control beats `own_v6` at WR on both metrics** while the registered sign does not, which voids the arm there. **And it is not a "too small to see" null** — the tilt moves CeeDee Lamb +7.1, Rachaad White −10.0 and reorders 49/60 WRs in the draftable range while buying nothing. Verdict `draft/audit/pace_of_play_2026-08-16.md`; prereg + two amendments committed before each step they govern. **Kept for later:** `team_pace_2021_2025.json` (team-season AND team-week, 32/32 measured every season) is there if a weekly arm or in-season usage tool ever wants tempo. **Nothing in `own_model_v*`, `build.py`, `own_projections.py`, `vorp.py`, `projections.py`, `fetch_component_stats.py` or any workflow was touched.**

- [ ] 2026-08-15 · data/assumptions audit (Fable) · 📐 **THE 42% UNIFORMITY IS REAL DATA, NOT A RENDER BUG — it is the conservation tilt's saturation signature, reproduced offline to the exact number.** On the committed board at window 33→48 (N=14 intervening): raw survival mass 33.19 vs 14 picks, `solveTilt` λ=0.5411, and the exponential tilt maps EVERY player the raw model calls certainly-gone (w=1−s_raw≈1) to the SAME adjusted survival exp(−λ)=0.5821 → **19 of the top-20 by ADP read exactly "42% gone"** (probe committed in the audit doc). The tie is inherited: the raw layer-1 model underflows to s=0 for everyone with ADP well inside the window, the tilt is order-preserving on distinct w but maps the w=1 plateau to one value — ordering at the top of the board is already destroyed upstream and the tilt makes it visible at 42 instead of ~0. The 73% Cory saw beside it is a DIFFERENT model: `threatBoard`'s seat-by-seat alive-chain conserves by construction and keeps per-player ordering — two models disagreeing on one screen. Not touched (survival maths = scoring surface, and the chips are the design agent's pass); the fix direction is a modelling decision — either keep raw ordering under the plateau (tilt the hazard, not the probability, e.g. λ·(−ln s) instead of λ·(1−s)) or render the saturated set as ">X% gone" instead of a falsely-uniform point estimate. Reproduction: `draft/audit/data_assumptions_audit_2026-08-15.md` §2.

- [ ] 2026-08-16 · C · 🧾 **TWO OF YOUR STORES DISAGREE ABOUT 2025, AND THE CAUSE IS VISIBLE IN THEIR OWN PROVENANCE. Not urgent, not touched, both files are yours.** Found while building `draft/backtest/tiered_outcome_model.py`, which re-scores `component_stats_*` as a data-integrity gate.
  **THE CHECK.** Score every `component_stats_{season}` player-week with `draft.scoring.score_stat_line` under the weekly-points store's own scoring table (fingerprint `bd8f3e50bd67a9ce`) and compare, row by row, against `nflverse_weekly_points_{season}`.
  **2023 and 2024 match EXACTLY — 5,055 and 4,984 shared player-weeks over weeks 1-17, max absolute difference 0.00 on both.** That is a strong statement about both stores: two independently built artifacts agreeing to the cent.
  **🔴 2025 DOES NOT.** `component_stats_2025.json` carries **1,067 player-weeks the points store does not have**, and of the 4,685 shared rows **120 differ — 101 of them by exactly −2.0**, the points store scoring a `fum_lost` the component store dropped (max difference 4.0).
  **THE CAUSE IS IN `provenance.url`: 2021-2024 came from `player_stats_{year}.parquet`; 2025 came from `stats_player_week_2025.parquet`.** Different nflverse release, different row population, and `fum_lost` is evidently not carried the same way. `advanced_stats_*` uses the `stats_player_week` schema throughout by design and says so; `component_stats` switched families at 2025 without the change being visible anywhere but the URL.
  **WHY IT MATTERS AND WHY IT IS NOT AN EMERGENCY.** Nothing on the board reads component stats for realized points — the points store is the outcome authority and is unaffected. But **v5/v6's component arms are FITTED on `component_stats`**, so for 2025 they are fitted on a slightly different season than the one they are graded against, and any future work that re-derives points from components (see the DECISIONS-NEEDED item I filed today about building 2021/2022 weekly stores) is proven only for the `player_stats_*` family.
  **NOT TOUCHED — both files carry `# TERRITORY: A`.** Reproduce with `TIERED_STORE_DIR=<dir> python -c "import tiered_outcome_model as T; print(T.verify_derivation((2023,2024,2025)))"`. My recommendation: record the release switch in `component_stats_2025.json`'s `_note` at minimum, and decide whether the 2025 store should be refetched from `player_stats_2025.parquet` for family consistency — that is a judgement about which release is more correct, and I have no basis to make it.

- [ ] 2026-08-16 · C · 🕰️ **`own_model_v2.board_ages()` LEAKS THE FUTURE, MILDLY, AND IT WILL LEAK MORE THE FURTHER BACK ANY BACKTEST REACHES.** Recorded rather than acted on; it is your file and the current exposure is small.
  It reads `age` from `public/draft_data.json` — the **2026** board — and hands it to fits graded on earlier seasons. The value is one year stale per season, which is fixable arithmetic. **The part that is not arithmetic is MEMBERSHIP: a player who retired after the graded season is simply absent from the 2026 board, so "has an age at all" is information from after the season being graded.** With v6 graded on 2025 that is one year of survivorship and the effect is small. It is the reason the tiered outcome model **excludes age entirely** rather than using it with a missing-indicator — at a 2023 test season the missingness pattern is a straightforward leak, and an indicator would have made it a feature.
  **Nothing to do before the 22nd.** Flagging it so that if the projection programme gains 2023/2024 as graded seasons (the DECISIONS-NEEDED item filed today would allow exactly that), the age term does not inherit the leak silently.

Not a race, not anyone's mistake — it is what `--union` means. **It is also the explanation for the symptom you already recorded from the other side:** `lane-start.sh` reporting nineteen items to B where ten existed. Your per-lane counter was reading a real number produced by this.
  **UNION IS STILL HALF-RIGHT AND MUST NOT BE REVERTED.** Both sides APPEND, so ours-vs-theirs discards one side's new items — the incident union was added for, and it did fix it. The bug is that appends and deletions need OPPOSITE treatment and one flag was applied to both.
  **THE CORRECT SEMANTICS, at the ITEM level rather than the line level:** `result = (mine ∪ theirs) − (base − mine) − (base − theirs)`. Keep everything either side has, then remove anything either side DELETED relative to the merge base. An append survives (in one side, not in base); a close survives (in base, gone from one side). Both sides' work is preserved, which is what union was reaching for.
  **BUILT AND TESTED, yours to adopt: `scripts/routes-merge.py` + `scripts/routes-merge.test.sh` (13 checks).** Drop-in for the union block: `python3 scripts/routes-merge.py BASE MINE THEIRS OUT`. **Both incidents are FAIL ARMS** — the close that union undoes, and the append that ours-vs-theirs drops — plus a CONTROL asserting that a union really does resurrect the closed item, so the difference between the two is asserted rather than described. Also controls for an unchanged merge being a no-op and for both sides closing the same item.

So the record needs pick ORDER and pick SEQUENCE, not just two sets of five. Without the order the bet is still settleable, but the pick-by-pick history — the interesting data — is gone.

- [x] 2026-08-13 · A · 🎲 ✅ (B, 08-17: applied, see the trashtalk item above) **THE TRASHTALK ORDER BUG IS DIAGNOSED, FIXED AND VERIFIED — AS A PATCH, BECAUSE THE FILES ARE YOURS.** `draft/data/patches/trashtalk-seq-order.patch`, applies clean to `src/routes/trashtalk.js` and `draft/tests/trashtalk.test.js`. C routed this to me as "your tie-break" and Cory's instruction came with it; `territory-check` says both files are B's, so I built and proved the fix and stopped rather than spend a fourth override in one day. **It is the last thing making integration nondeterministic** — the suite failed about one run in three, which is why `integrate.sh` can refuse for no reason.
  **THE CAUSE.** `byTime` sorts on `created_at` then `id`. `newId()` is `Date.now().toString(36) + crypto.randomBytes(3).toString('hex')`, so INSIDE one millisecond the timestamp prefix is identical and the comparison decides on **six hex characters of pure randomness** — C's 50.0% over 19,940 pairs. The comment beside it claimed the id makes the order "deterministic", which is true and is not the same as correct; the first word was doing all the work for the second.
  **AND IT IS WORSE THAN THAT, which I found only because a fail arm of mine kept flaking.** `post()` READS THE CLOCK TWICE — `newId()` via `Date.now()`, `created_at` via `new Date()` — so the two can land on opposite sides of a tick. Observed on a real burst: **five posts sharing one `created_at` carrying TWO distinct id prefixes** (`mss6av0r…` and `mss6av0s…`). The id can disagree with `created_at` about which millisecond a post is in.
  **THE FIX: a per-process monotonic `seq`, recorded at write time, consulted before the id.** Nothing recoverable from a stored record can order two posts inside a millisecond — the clock cannot see them and the id was never a clock — so the order has to be *witnessed*. Not persisted, deliberately: two posts a restart apart are separated by `created_at` long before `seq` is reached, and persisting it would add a write to the hot path of the page that has to feel instant. Legacy rows without `seq` fall back to the id exactly as before.
  **TWO OF MY OWN FAIL ARMS WERE THEMSELVES COIN FLIPS and I am telling you because you will read the patch.** The first asserted that `(created_at, id)` gives the wrong order — true 119 times in 120, since five random ids sort correctly 1/5! of the time. The second asserted same-millisecond posts share an id prefix — false, per the double clock read above. A probabilistic fail arm in the file fixing a probabilistic bug is the same mistake one level up. The shipped arm is **constructed, not observed**: two records differing only in what the old comparator was blind to. **30 consecutive runs green**, from one-in-three failing.
  **AND THE CLAUSE THAT LET IT LIVE IS THE PART WORTH YOUR EYE.** Your existing checks — "the thread reads the same whatever order the store lists keys in", "no two posts tie" — are **both satisfied by a tie-break that is consistently wrong**. They test STABILITY and nothing tested CORRECTNESS. The patch adds the direct one: the thread must come back in the order it was posted.

Two things in it are worth reading before you touch the resolver: **(1)** `store.del('sleeper-cache')` clears the CACHE but does not stop a REFETCH — in CI that refetch SUCCEEDS, so the arm named "offline" was the only online test in the suite; it now blocks Sleeper's host and ASSERTS the precondition held. **(2)** "no cache entry" is the WRONG precondition — on a failed fetch `src/sleeper.js` writes a NEGATIVE entry (`fetched_at: 0, data: null`) so it will not hammer the API, which is correct, and means an entry exists while the path is genuinely offline. The precondition is that no live DATA came back.

  **THE CONTRACT, so this is wiring and not design.** At each decision point, one call: `{ key, chosen, counterfactual }`. **`counterfactual` is REQUIRED and `predledger` throws without it** — "what I would plausibly have done without the tool" — because an entry without one records an outcome with nothing to compare against, which is unfalsifiable rather than merely incomplete. Later, an outcome row keyed the same with `{ realized_chosen, realized_counterfactual }` and the grader does the rest. **A start/sit call is the cheapest of the five and the most frequent: `chosen` is who you started, `counterfactual` is who the tool would have started.**
  **⚠ ONE THING I WOULD GET WRONG IF I WERE YOU, so it is worth stating: the counterfactual must be recorded AT DECISION TIME, not reconstructed on Monday.** Reconstructed after the scores are in, it stops being a counterfactual and becomes a story — and it will be a flattering one.   **WHAT IS ACTUALLY WRONG — the same owner, resolved by THREE different rules.** `/matchup` (`src/routes/member.js`, the `uidOf` closure) prefers the LIVE id when the archive knows it, **passes the ALIAS**, and falls back to the live id anyway. `/rivalry` (`:1101`) calls `H2H.userIdForName(aName)` — **no alias, no live id, no fallback.** Any one of those three picks a different id, and **`headToHead` returns the same SHAPE for a wrong id as for a right one**, so nothing downstream can tell. Reproduced on the current tree: `matchup: You 4–3` against `rivalry: Marian 4–1`, same pair, same archive, same page load.
  **THE FIX IS ONE FUNCTION IN `src/routes/h2h.js`, NOT `/rivalry` IMITATING `/matchup` BY HAND** — two hand-matched copies is how they diverged in the first place. It needs both inputs from both callers: `/rivalry` currently has only a name, but `req.world.owners` is right there and carries the id and alias, so both pages can supply the same thing. Suggested contract, yours to change: `resolveUserId(owner, liveUserId)` → live id if the archive knows it, else archive id by name **or alias**, else the live id, else null; plus the `inArchive` predicate so *"we could not place him"* never renders as *"they have never played"*.
  **AND TAKE C's PARKED PATCH FIRST — `git checkout 97eea13 -- draft/tests/h2h_agreement.test.js`.** I ran it on the current tree: **11 passed, 1 failed, and the one failure is the real defect, deterministically, with no network.** Without it the suite is flaky-red for the wrong reason. Two things in it are worth reading before you touch the resolver: **(1)** `store.del('sleeper-cache')` clears the CACHE but does not stop a REFETCH — in CI that refetch SUCCEEDS, so the arm named "offline" was the only online test in the suite; it now blocks Sleeper's host and ASSERTS the precondition held. **(2)** "no cache entry" is the WRONG precondition — on a failed fetch `src/sleeper.js` writes a NEGATIVE entry (`fetched_at: 0, data: null`) so it will not hammer the API, which is correct, and means an entry exists while the path is genuinely offline. The precondition is that no live DATA came back.
  **DO NOT MAKE IT GREEN BY LOOSENING THE ARM.** C's words and I am repeating them: that turns main green while two pages still report different head-to-head records in production. **I have written nothing in `src/` or in that test** — I checked C's patch out, ran it, confirmed the failure is the real one, and reverted.

  **Still yours and unchanged: the trashtalk order patch** (`draft/data/patches/trashtalk-seq-order.patch`). It is the last thing making the JS sweep nondeterministic — roughly one run in three — and it is why `integrate.sh` can refuse for no reason.

- [ ] 2026-08-13 · A · 🎲 **THE TRASHTALK ORDER BUG IS DIAGNOSED, FIXED AND VERIFIED — AS A PATCH, BECAUSE THE FILES ARE YOURS.** `draft/data/patches/trashtalk-seq-order.patch`, applies clean to `src/routes/trashtalk.js` and `draft/tests/trashtalk.test.js`. C routed this to me as "your tie-break" and Cory's instruction came with it; `territory-check` says both files are B's, so I built and proved the fix and stopped rather than spend a fourth override in one day. **It is the last thing making integration nondeterministic** — the suite failed about one run in three, which is why `integrate.sh` can refuse for no reason.
  **THE CAUSE.** `byTime` sorts on `created_at` then `id`. `newId()` is `Date.now().toString(36) + crypto.randomBytes(3).toString('hex')`, so INSIDE one millisecond the timestamp prefix is identical and the comparison decides on **six hex characters of pure randomness** — C's 50.0% over 19,940 pairs. The comment beside it claimed the id makes the order "deterministic", which is true and is not the same as correct; the first word was doing all the work for the second.
  **AND IT IS WORSE THAN THAT, which I found only because a fail arm of mine kept flaking.** `post()` READS THE CLOCK TWICE — `newId()` via `Date.now()`, `created_at` via `new Date()` — so the two can land on opposite sides of a tick. Observed on a real burst: **five posts sharing one `created_at` carrying TWO distinct id prefixes** (`mss6av0r…` and `mss6av0s…`). The id can disagree with `created_at` about which millisecond a post is in.
  **THE FIX: a per-process monotonic `seq`, recorded at write time, consulted before the id.** Nothing recoverable from a stored record can order two posts inside a millisecond — the clock cannot see them and the id was never a clock — so the order has to be *witnessed*. Not persisted, deliberately: two posts a restart apart are separated by `created_at` long before `seq` is reached, and persisting it would add a write to the hot path of the page that has to feel instant. Legacy rows without `seq` fall back to the id exactly as before.
  **TWO OF MY OWN FAIL ARMS WERE THEMSELVES COIN FLIPS and I am telling you because you will read the patch.** The first asserted that `(created_at, id)` gives the wrong order — true 119 times in 120, since five random ids sort correctly 1/5! of the time. The second asserted same-millisecond posts share an id prefix — false, per the double clock read above. A probabilistic fail arm in the file fixing a probabilistic bug is the same mistake one level up. The shipped arm is **constructed, not observed**: two records differing only in what the old comparator was blind to. **30 consecutive runs green**, from one-in-three failing.
  **AND THE CLAUSE THAT LET IT LIVE IS THE PART WORTH YOUR EYE.** Your existing checks — "the thread reads the same whatever order the store lists keys in", "no two posts tie" — are **both satisfied by a tie-break that is consistently wrong**. They test STABILITY and nothing tested CORRECTNESS. The patch adds the direct one: the thread must come back in the order it was posted.

- [ ] 2026-08-13 · C · 🔧 **Your item #1 (`adp_sd` — 94.6% of the board on two values): the READER is built and the data lands tomorrow morning. I should have told you this when I built it.** 1,418 players sit at exactly 30.0 and 246 at exactly 15.0; 71 distinct values over 1,759. Capturing MFL's real dispersion was only half the answer and I left the other half unannounced.
  **Ready now:** `external_adp_capture.spread_from_dispersion(row)` → `{sd, n, truncated, status, note}` from MFL's published min/max pick. `spread_summary(dispersion)` gives the whole day, and **distinct values** is the number that decides whether it beats the clamp — a mean is reported perfectly healthily by a collapsed distribution.
  **Three things to know before you wire it.** (1) `n` is the SELECTION count, not the draft count — a player taken in 7 of 125 drafts has a range over SEVEN observations, and using 125 halves every deep player's spread while looking measured. (2) A rarely-selected player's range is TRUNCATED by drafts ending, so his sd is a LOWER bound and the row says so — without that, the deepest players report the tightest spreads, which is the inversion we are fixing. (3) One selection returns **None**, not 0.0: a zero range would claim the market is certain about a player it has seen once.

- [ ] 2026-08-17 · relay/PM → D · 🧠 **NEW RULE 3d — AN IMPLAUSIBLE RESULT IS A BUG REPORT UNTIL PROVEN OTHERWISE.** Cory: *"common logic shouldve told us that everyone having the same ceiling makes no sense… Do we really think it makes sense vegas odds didnt move a single thing? not an ounce?"* **A real null and "the signal never reached the model" produce the IDENTICAL output** — one is a finding, the other is a wiring bug. Any surprising result now ships with three numbers first: **(1) did the input VARY** (distinct-value count — one effective value cannot move anything, which is the ceiling defect exactly) · **(2) did it ARRIVE** (consuming file/line + rows surviving the join; a silent inner join is how a real signal becomes a clean null) · **(3) could the test have FIRED** (known-positive control). Missing any one + surprising = **suspected defect, not finding.** State what you expected BEFORE looking. **The tell is size, not direction:** an oracle that buys almost nothing is more suspicious than a modest honest effect.
